# 25. MachineConfigPool

> **Track:** OpenShift Prerequisites for Red Hat OpenShift AI
> **Level:** Corporate/L3 — designed for an engineer with 10+ years of Linux, Kubernetes, OpenShift, networking, storage, and operations experience
> **Delivery model:** architecture + implementation project + validation + operations + troubleshooting + interview readiness
> **Version note:** Validate the exact Red Hat supported-configuration matrix before implementation or upgrade. Examples are learning patterns, not a substitute for the support matrix.

## 1. Executive summary

Group nodes for host-level configuration, track rendered configs, coordinate reboots, detect drift, pause updates, and isolate specialized pools.

**Why this matters to OpenShift AI:** GPU, high-performance networking, proxy trust, registries, kernel arguments, and OS-level tuning can affect AI nodes. Host changes must be declarative and upgrade-safe.

### 1.1 Senior-engineer outcomes
1. Explain MachineConfigPool from API, controller, node, workload, and operational perspectives.
2. Design a production pattern for MachineConfigPool with clear ownership and failure domains.
3. Implement the pattern from scratch with declarative resources and `oc` validation.
4. Assess how MachineConfigPool affects OpenShift AI notebooks, pipelines, model serving, and accelerators.
5. Troubleshoot admission, reconciliation, scheduling, networking, storage, and upgrade symptoms.
6. Produce change evidence, rollback steps, acceptance criteria, and an operations handover.

## 3. Production architecture

### 3.1 Availability
- Architecture goal: make machineconfigpool predictable for availability.
- Design decision: define the supported configuration, owner, API boundary, and evidence source.
- Failure domain: identify whether a failure is cluster-wide, node-pool-wide, namespace-scoped, or workload-scoped.

## 4. Project from scratch

### Project title: Create a dedicated GPU MachineConfigPool and perform a controlled configuration rollout

### 4.1 Scenario
Your enterprise is preparing a production Red Hat OpenShift AI platform. The architecture board requires evidence that **MachineConfigPool** is designed, implemented, validated, secured, monitored, and recoverable.

### 4.2 Deliverables
1. Current-state discovery report
2. Target-state architecture
3. Assumptions and constraints
4. Support-matrix evidence
5. Implementation manifests
6. Command transcript
7. Risk assessment
8. Security review
9. Capacity model
10. Monitoring plan
11. Failure-injection results
12. Rollback plan
13. Operations handover
14. Acceptance sign-off

### 4.3 Phase 0 — change preparation
1. Identify business owner and technical owner.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
2. Define maintenance window and blast radius.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
3. Export current resources before modification.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
4. Capture ClusterVersion and ClusterOperator health.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
5. Confirm etcd backup and restore procedure.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
6. Confirm cluster-admin break-glass access.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
7. Validate DNS, NTP, proxy, registry, storage, and ingress dependencies.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
8. Confirm OpenShift AI and Operator compatibility.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
9. Define measurable success and rollback thresholds.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.
10. Open a change record and attach peer review.
   - Evidence: record command output, timestamp, operator, and result.

### 4.4 Phase 1 — discovery
```bash
oc get mcp
```
- Record expected output, actual output, anomalies, and follow-up action.
```bash
oc describe mcp <pool>
```
- Record expected output, actual output, anomalies, and follow-up action.
```bash
oc get mc
```
- Record expected output, actual output, anomalies, and follow-up action.
```bash
oc get nodes -l machineconfiguration.openshift.io/role=<pool>
```

### 4.5 Phase 2 — lab namespace and governance baseline
```yaml
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: ai-machineconfigpool-lab
  labels:
    platform.example.com/owner: ai-platform
    platform.example.com/environment: lab
    platform.example.com/topic: machineconfigpool
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: ServiceAccount
metadata:
  name: machineconfigpool-runner
  namespace: ai-machineconfigpool-lab
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: ResourceQuota
metadata:
  name: machineconfigpool-quota
  namespace: ai-machineconfigpool-lab
spec:
  hard:
    requests.cpu: "4"
    requests.memory: 16Gi
    limits.cpu: "8"
    limits.memory: 32Gi
    requests.storage: 100Gi
    pods: "20"
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: LimitRange
metadata:
  name: machineconfigpool-defaults
  namespace: ai-machineconfigpool-lab
spec:
  limits:
  - type: Container
    defaultRequest:
      cpu: 250m
      memory: 512Mi
    default:
      cpu: "1"
      memory: 2Gi
```

Apply and verify:
```bash
oc apply -f 00-machineconfigpool-baseline.yaml
oc get all,resourcequota,limitrange -n ai-machineconfigpool-lab
```

## 5. Security and governance
1. As the **platform architect**, verify that machineconfigpool follows least privilege and preserves availability.
   - Confirm who can read, create, update, patch, delete, approve, and impersonate.
   - Confirm secrets are not committed in plaintext or exposed in logs.
   - Confirm images and Operator sources are trusted and auditable.
   - Confirm namespace and node boundaries are intentional.
   - Record an exception owner and expiry date for every deviation.
2. As the **OpenShift administrator**, verify that machineconfigpool follows least privilege and preserves capacity.
   - Confirm who can read, create, update, patch, delete, approve, and impersonate.

## 6. Capacity and performance
1. Capacity test 1: measure **memory working set** while exercising machineconfigpool in the test environment.

   - Apply controlled load.
   - Record p50, p95, p99, errors, saturation, and recovery time.
   - Compare against the SLO and scaling threshold.
   - Document the safe operating envelope.

## 8. Troubleshooting decision tree
### Scenario 1: Incorrect Label in development
**Symptom:** A workload or controller related to machineconfigpool does not reach the expected state.
**Initial hypothesis:** `incorrect label`.
1. Confirm the user-visible symptom and exact timestamp.
2. Identify the namespace, resource kind, name, UID, owner references, and generation.
3. Compare `.metadata.generation` with `.status.observedGeneration` when available.
4. Inspect conditions, events, admission messages, and recent changes.
5. Identify the reconciling controller and inspect its logs.
6. Check node readiness, scheduling constraints, quotas, limits, SCC, and RBAC.
7. Check image, registry, DNS, network, route, service, endpoint, and certificate dependencies.
8. Check StorageClass, PVC, PV, CSI, topology, mount, and expansion dependencies.
9. Check Operator Subscription, InstallPlan, CSV, catalog, ClusterOperator, and MCP health.
10. Change one variable only; collect before-and-after evidence.
11. Verify recovery from the user perspective and platform perspective.
12. Record root cause, corrective action, preventive action, owner, and target date.

### Scenario 2: Missing Permission in test
**Symptom:** A workload or controller related to machineconfigpool does not reach the expected state.
**Initial hypothesis:** `missing permission`.
1. Confirm the user-visible symptom and exact timestamp.
2. Identify the namespace, resource kind, name, UID, owner references, and generation.
3. Compare `.metadata.generation` with `.status.observedGeneration` when available.
4. Inspect conditions, events, admission messages, and recent changes.
5. Identify the reconciling controller and inspect its logs.
6. Check node readiness, scheduling constraints, quotas, limits, SCC, and RBAC.
7. Check image, registry, DNS, network, route, service, endpoint, and certificate dependencies.
8. Check StorageClass, PVC, PV, CSI, topology, mount, and expansion dependencies.
9. Check Operator Subscription, InstallPlan, CSV, catalog, ClusterOperator, and MCP health.
10. Change one variable only; collect before-and-after evidence.
11. Verify recovery from the user perspective and platform perspective.
12. Record root cause, corrective action, preventive action, owner, and target date.

### Scenario 3: Unsupported Version in staging
**Symptom:** A workload or controller related to machineconfigpool does not reach the expected state.

## 9. Failure-injection labs
### Lab 1: Inject and recover from stale cache
**Objective:** Learn how `stale cache` manifests in machineconfigpool without affecting production.
**Safety:** Run only in a disposable lab namespace or non-production cluster.
1. Capture the healthy baseline.
2. Export all resources that will be changed.
3. Introduce a controlled condition representing `stale cache`.
4. Observe admission output, events, conditions, logs, metrics, and user impact.
5. Follow the troubleshooting decision tree without skipping evidence collection.
6. Restore the original declarative state.
7. Verify controller convergence and workload recovery.
8. Calculate detection time, diagnosis time, repair time, and total impact.
9. Add an alert, policy, test, or validation gate to prevent recurrence.
10. Attach the evidence to the lab report.

## 10. Corporate operational runbooks
### Runbook 1: MachineConfigPool operational action 1
- Trigger: define the alert, ticket, planned change, or user report.
- Scope: identify affected cluster, node pool, namespace, and AI service.
- Preconditions: verify access, backup, change approval, and current health.
- Diagnosis: execute read-only discovery before remediation.
- Remediation: use declarative changes and preserve rollback data.
- Validation: test platform state and end-user behavior.
- Escalation: specify vendor, platform, security, network, storage, or application owner.
- Closure: document root cause and prevention.

## 16. Final senior-engineer summary
A Corporate/L3 engineer should treat **MachineConfigPool** as an operated system, not a one-time command. The implementation is complete only when desired state, reconciliation, security, capacity, observability, failure recovery, upgrade compatibility, rollback, and ownership are all proven.
